Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
2212. JOHNSON GILLIGHEN was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BOLLAND conducted the prosecution.
JAMES NICHOLLS. I am a draper, and live in Charlotte-street, Whitechapel. On the 1st of October I was at the Bell public-house, Old Bailey; the prisoner came in, went into the parlour, and called for porter and bread and cheese; nobody else was there; he called for some salt and mustard, and quarrelled with the boy for handing the mustard in the way he did; he drank the beer, came out with the bread and cheese in his hand, and said he would go to the next house; he returned presently, and said he had been ill-used at the other house. (I did not see him with any bundle;) he then asked for a bundle; inquiry was made whether he had left one there, and nobody knew any thing of it; he wanted to go into the parlour; I stopped him, and twice put him out of the street door; a gentleman who lives on Ludgate-hill, came in presently; he went into the parlour, and called for a pint of beer; the landlady went in with it, and on coming out, she looked round the parlour, near the door where the prisoner had sat, and saw something lay; she took it up, and put it into my hand; it was a blue handkerchief, twisted up; I took it to the bar, untwisted it, and saw it was money. I went and asked the prisoner what he had lost; he said a blue handkerchief - I asked what was in it; he said he did not like to say; I said, "You ought to tell;" he said he would not tell me. I went and pushed him into the parlour, and said again, "What have you lost?" he said, "I don't like to tell you." I asked what was the value of it; he said there was 3l. in it - I said (in the presence of two or three persons, who had followed me into the parlour), "Is this yours?" he said Yes; I said, "Is what is in it yours?" he said Yes - I then said, "It is counterfeit coin, and I shall detain you;" he then tried to escape, but was detained till the officer came; there were seventy-nine counterfeit shillings in the handkerchief.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHER. Q. Do you carry on business on your own account? A. Yes; I am often at the bell; I was not in the parlour when the prisoner went out; he asked the landlady for his bundle; he was partly drunk, but capable of knowing what he was about, and walking about. I saw the landlady pick the bundle off the seat where he had sat, and feeling something in it, she put it into my hand; the money was so bad, every body could tell immediately that it was counterfeit; I would not have taken it; I should have refused it at first sight.
MR. BOLLAND. Q. Were you present at the different times he asked for the bundle? A. Yes; he asked the landlady, the servant, and myself.
JOHN CHARLES BARRELL. I am a broker, and live at the Bell. On the night of the 1st of October I came home at a quarter to ten o'clock, and went into the parlour; the prisoner was there, crying; he said he had lost his property; Nicholls was standing at his side, with this handkerchief in his hand - the prisoner said it was a blue handkerchief - I said to Nicholls, "Give the poor fellow his handkerchief;" he said, "It is this coin;" I then said,"I must fetch an officer" - I went to the door - an offices was coming by - I called him in - the prisoner had before that said he would not go without his handkerchief, andthat it contained about 3l. - he was given in charge - the officer found, I think, 1s. 6d. in good money on him - he said, "I am done."
Cross-examined. Q. He was crying? A. Yes; he seemed anxious to recover the parcel - he heard me say I must fetch an officer.
GEORGE LOCK. I am an officer. I was called in, and found the prisoner in custody - Nicholls gave me this coin in this handkerchief, as it is now - I found 1s. 6d. in good money, and 8d. in copper on him.
JOHN FIELD. I am an inspector of coin at the Mint - the whole of this is counterfeit - it is what is termed cast money, made of white or Britannia metal, such as spoons are made of.
MR. CHURCHER addressed the Court on behalf of the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 45.
Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18271025-236]
2213. MARY MURRAY was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BOLLAND conducted the prosecution.
JOHN CHESTER. I keep the Fortune of War public-house, in Giltspur-street . On the 16th of October the prisoner came to my house a little before ten o'clock in the morning - she asked for a small glass of gin and milk- I served her - she put down a piece of base metal in the shape of a sixpence - I looked at it, and asked where she got it - she said in change from a neighbour - I asked if she had any more - she said what did I mean by that - I said, "I suspect you have" - she said, "Why, that is not a bad one" - she snatched it up and went out - I followed, and brought her back; she was then in company with another woman.
GEORGE HAZLEWOOD WORRALL. I am an officer. I was sent for, and found a few articles in her pocket, but no base money; I then shook her stays, and this purse dropped from her back, with fifteen bad shillings in it; I asked where she got them; she said a friend gave them to her, and she thought she might get something by them; I have had them ever since; they have been turned about before the Grand Jury; they looked better before; I found one good sixpence in her pocket.
JOHN FIELD. These are all counterfeit, and cast metal; they were bright when I saw them at Guildhall, but they soon Iarnish.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that she met one Mary Millins, who gave her the purse to hold for her.
GUILTY. Aged 55.
Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18271025-237]
2214. MARIA DONOVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of October, 13 yards of ribbon, value 8s., the goods of James Powell.
WILLIAM WATSON. I am shopman to James Powell, a haberdasher, of Holborn-bridge. On the 24th of October, between five and six o'clock, the prisoner came in, and asked to see some ribbons; Upton showed her some; I saw her take a piece out with her left hand, and put it under her shawl; she then said, "None of these will do;" he then showed her another drawer; she said none of them would do; she then said she had been there that afternoon, and laid out 15s., which I knew was false; she was going out, and when she had got about half a yard from the counter, I told her to leave the ribbon which she had got; it then dropped from her on the floor; she had a child with her; a man came in at that moment, and asked me for some stockings; I said, "I will show you some in a minute;" she said to the child, "We must look after your father, my boy;" and ran out of the shop; I was going out after her, and the man who had come in, gave me a knock on the head, and knocked me down; he then passed me, and ran up Plumbtree-court after her, and as he ran up there he struck a woman, and cut her lip.
RICHARD UPTON. I am servant to Mr. Powell, and showed the prisoner some ribbons; I saw her attempt to take another piece, but seeing my eyes fixed on her, she placed it in the drawer again; I ran after her, and the constable took her; she was taken to a public-house, and rescued from the constable, but taken again.
THOMAS GREEN. I am a constable; I heard the alarm, and secured the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I am very sorry; I was much in liquor, and hope you will be mereiful.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18271025-238]
2215. WILLIAM FRANCIS was indicted for feloniously assaulting John Stapp, on the King's highway, on the 22d of September, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 25l.; 1 chain, value 8l.; 2 seals, value 2l., and 1 key, value 5s., his property.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
MR. JOHN STAPP. I am a cheesemonger, and live on Snowhill. On the 22d of September, near nine o'clock in the evening, I was walking from a warehouse, which I have in Smithfield, down King-street, towards home; I had a gold watch, with chain and seals about me, and as I came to the corner of Fox and Knot-court, which leads to the George public-house, I saw a man standing on the pavement, just at the gateway; he took me by the shoulder, and pushed me with his arm some way down the court (it was a wet night - it did not rain, but was dirty - I am sure of that - it had rained in the course of the day); the prisoner then seized me on the right, and pressed me towards the other man, who first assailed me; I was impounded between them; a third man then came up, and took my watch from my pocket with such force, that the case opened, and flew ten or twelve yards; the person who had stolen the watch ran down the court; I called Stop thief! as soon as I could, and attempted to pursue the thief; the prisoner prevented me, and said, "Is any thing the matter, Sir?" he stood across me, before my breast, and when the prisoner let me pass, the man on my leftalso said, "Is any thing the matter?" and he stopped me in the same way; he said,"Is any thing the matter, Sir?" as cool as could be; I went on, and pursued the man, who had taken my watch, till I came to the corner of West-street, and there lost sight of him; I have not seen my watch since; I had seen the prisoner in the neighbourhood of Smithfield before - I have no doubt of his being the person, for, as he stood before me, there was a gas-light behind me; he had a smockfrock on, which covered his clothes; this was on Saturdaynight, and I gave a description of him on Tuesday, at the Mansion-house. I saw the prisoner there, and swore clearly and positively to him.
COURT. Q. How long afterwards was he taken into custody? A. I think on the Tuesday or Wednesday morning.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHER. Q. Be so kind as to recollect exactly at what time this was? A. About nine o'clock, rather before than after - it was was very near nine - it was dark, of course - it had been raining, and was a damp dirty night.
Q. I believe the lamps do not, on a damp night, give so much light as on another? A. I believe not; there was a gas-light at the bottom of the court, which is about sixty yards long - that was not the light I saw him by; there is a light at Mr. Yarrow's, a grocer, at the corner of the yard- I was rather flurried.
Q. You are rather a nervous man? A. That made me nervous; I have never been right since; I saw him as plainly as I do you, Sir; the moment he seized my arm, I was convinced I had seen him before - it did not last more than a minute, I suppose, or a minute and a half; I was not looking at him entirely; when I attempted to pursue the man, the prisoner stopped me - he came breast to breast to me; I had rather passed him; he came and stood before me - I was between the two men, and the light was, as it were, overhead, a little distance behind me - it was only the little distance they had driven me down the court.
NICHOLAS YARROW. I live in King-street, Snow-hill, and am a grocer. On the night of the 22d of September my attention was called to this yard; I was in my own shop, and saw a scuffle at the top of the yard - I went out; when I got to my door, which might take me about a minute, the parties had dispersed; I went down the yard, on hearing a cry of Stop thief! from a faint voice, and met two men coming up the yard - one was in a white smock-frock, the other had plain clothes of some description; I spoke to the man in the smock-frock, as he was the nearest of the two to me - he was quite close to me; I asked him what was the matter - he said a gentleman had been robbed of his watch; I inquired if the thief was taken - he said, "He is not taken; we have been pursuing him, and he has made his escape through these courts."
Q. Did this pass in such a light, that you could well see the features of the person in the smock-frock? A. Yes; I firmly believe the prisoner to be the man - I had not seen him before to my knowledge; I did not recollect him at the time; I have no doubt about his being the man.
COURT. Q. Had you any doubt of him when he was taken into custody? A. No - and I have now no doubt.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHER. Q. Did you not express a doubt? A. No; I said I had not the slightest doubt he was the man, for he resembled him as much as two men could resemble each other; I knew him by his face and figure - but I was more positive to his face, as he had at first a smock-frock on.
Q. When two men are dressed very much alike, may they not be easily mistaken in a dark night? A. This was not a very dark night; I cannot remember whether I heard him speak at the Mansion-house.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you take a very accurate inspection of him at the Mansion-house before you swore to him? A. Yes; I said I should know him better with his hat on - it was put on, and I had no doubt of him.
MR. CHURCHER. Q. Could you have sworn to him without his hat? A. I think I could; the robbery happened between half-past eight and nine o'clock.
JURY. Q. Did you see Mr. Stapp there? A. No, not at that time; but in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour, one of his men came to my house to borrow a candle, to look for the watch-case - that was the first of my hearing that it was Mr. Stapp who was robbed.
COURT. Q. Did you believe the story the man in the smock-frock told you? A. Yes; and returned to my house; there was a very good light - there is a gas-light at the top of the court.
JURY. Q. What induced you to go to the Mansion-house? A. Mr. Stapp came with his man, and told me what had occurred; he knew me as a neighbour; I observed to him that a very able man ran after the thief, and he would have been of use - I said he was a very able man, in a smock-frock; Mr. Stapp said, "That is the very man:" he appeared extremely agitated - Mr. Cope fetched me to the Mansion-house.
CHARLES HERDSFIELD. I am an officer of the City. I apprehended the prisoner: Mr. Stapp described the man to me, and said he believed he was a drover; his description enabled me to find him.
MR. CHURCHER. Q. When you heard the description of the man, you said you did not think the prisoner was the man? A. I said there was another person very similar to him: that man has been apprehended since - he is not quite so stout; the prosecutor said the other was not the man - there is a resemblance between them.
Q. So great, that a person in great agitation might mistake one for the other? A. That I cannot say.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent, and have friends to prove it.
CHARLES WELLER. I live at Mr. White's, the Hope and Anchor public-house, Hereford-street, Paddington - I am pot-boy there. I remember the night of the 22d of September; the prisoner was there the best part of that day - I cannot say at what time he came; I first saw him about two or three o'clock in the afternoon; he was there, in and out, till a quarter after nine - he then left; he was there at a quarter-past nine; he was there, with others, on account of a man stealing some sheep; they were drinking beer, and were in and out, watching for the man who had stolen the sheep, to take him: they had some mutton-chops for supper.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How long had you been pot-boy there? A. About three months: three other persons were with him - one left in the afternoon, but the prisoner and the two others remained; I did not serve them with every thing they had - I served them with porter - they had nothing else to my recollection; I took in nothing but porter, they paid my master - I was then standing at the tap-room door - I cannot say how much it was; there was no dispute about it, and I did not hear what it was - they all divided it, and paid their share. Thompson, a police officer, was with them - he is not here to-day; he was only once in the house; he was after the man; I saw Thompson there in the afternoon once; they did not dine there - they did not have any thing till he evening; the butcher in our neighbourhood brought the chops, I took the frying-pan in to them, and they cooked them themselves - it was on a Saturday.
Q. What sort of a day was it - it did not rain, did it? A. I was in the house, and do not go out; I did not notice whether people came in wet or splashed; I cannot tell where I was on the Saturday before, or what company were there then, there are so many people come to our house; I did not know the prisoner before: I can tell who came on the Saturday night afterwards - it was the prisoner's brother and Mr. Broom; they took me to the Mansion-house.
Q. What makes you particularly remember that this was the 22d of September? A. I do remember it, because the prisoner was there, and we do not have any cooking in our tap-room on Saturday nights; but I told master they had been there all day, and wanted something to eat.
Q. Why does that make you remember it was the 22d? A. I cannot tell - this is the 22d of October, I think; I do not know what date last Saturday was.
MR. CHURCHER. Q. Are you in the habit of observing your master when he takes people's reckoning? A. No; the officer was there in the afternoon; it might be a fine or a damp day for what I know; I observed the day very particularly; I attended at the Mansion-house on the Monday week, I believe - my attention was then directed to the day.
COURT. Q. Did you know Thompson before? A. Yes; he came in once; he was once with them about sheep; that was about the middle of the morning; he went out looking for the man, and they were in and out; he was not with them afterwards.
Q. What do you mean by saying he was with them? A. He brought them in; they all came in together - he did not stay more than five minutes; I only saw him once- that was about two or three o'clock; our door is kept open; I serve in the tap, and go to the cellar; I do not think it was wet; I do not know what occurred on the Saturday before - the prisoner's brother came on the Saturday after, and had conversation with me and my master; his brother was not there with him - I did not know him before.
JURY. Q. Is it your duty to serve in the bar, and go up and down stairs? A. Yes; I very seldom carry out beer or collect the pots; I do when there are a great many out; I then go out with the man.
GEORGE WELLER. I am nothing particular - the last place I lived at was a bake-house. I was at the Hope and Anchor on Saturday, the 22d of September; I went there about seven o'clock, and staid till about nine. I saw the prisoner there; I left him there at nine, and found him there at seven; they had mutton chops and roasted potatoes for supper.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What is your business? A. I am nothing particular; I went to assist at the baker's, Mr. Wells, Great James-street, Lisson-grove; I was about four months with him, and have had no place since; I was third hand with him, and carried out bread; I do not serve at the Hope and Anchor; I never saw the prisoner before; he sat in a box on the right hand side as you go in at the door; I was in front of the same box as the prisoner and his party - there were three of them. I had no share of their porter; there were three when I went in, and three when I left.
Q. What made you take so particular notice of the prisoner as to come here, to swear you knew him again? A. Because a man who was with the prisoner talked about some stolen sheep. I did not see the Police-officer - the chops were cooked in the tap-room; a man came in with the potatoes, and they bought them; I saw nobody else buy any; I went to the Mansion-house on the Tuesday; I do not know the date. I am certain of the 22d; I do not know what day of the month this is.
Q. How came you to be so well assured this was on the 22d of September? A. Because it was on the day I left my place; I was dismissed about the time I went there - I had been out that day with bread; I cannot say whether it was rainy or fine - there was no quarrel in the house while I was there; I live at No. 2, Union-street, Paddington; the prisoner's brother came to me, not to my house. I came into the Hope and Anchor while they were there, and they asked me if I was one who was there; I am sure this was on the 22d of September.
MR. CHURCHER. Q. It was Saturday night? A. Yes; I never saw them there before or after; the people of the public-house know me; I am the last witness' brother.
COURT. Q. Who do you work for now? A. I have been out of place ever since - I had no mutton-chops - I saw them eat them; they were broiled; one of the men who is here cooked them, with a gridiron - I think it was a gridiron - I did not get up to look - I cannot say whether it was a gridiron or saucepan - I was in the same box with them; there was a box between me and the fire-place - I could not well see the fire without getting up; a man sat at the end of the box - I saw no frying-pan or gridiron brought in; the potatoes were roasted; a man brought them in to sell, just as the chops were ready - they did not give me any - I was not known to them - I was with some other young men - I could not see the fire.
JURY. Q. How was the prisoner dressed? A. In the same dress he has now, except that he had a red handkerchief - I swear he was never out of the tap-room from seven to nine o'clock - I know the time I left the house, because I was going to the Crown and Apple Tree public-house, in Berwick-street, to see somebody - I slept at home - I have been there several nights since, and staid there a good many hours together - I usually go home about ten o'clock - I was there last night for about five minutes; the prisoner's brother was not with them, but the men who came with him were there, the night they had the chops, and those men asked me if I was there; they had sat in the same box with me; they asked if I was not the young man who was there - I was not surprised at the question.
COURT. Q. Did you know they were looking after sheep? A. I heard them talking about it - I did not know where they were stolen from; they were looking for the man; they knew him; he was a butcher up there - I did not think it strange they should remain so long in the house.
JOHN SELLER. I am a journeyman baker. I was at the Hope and Anchor on Saturday, the 22d of September; I went there about seven o'clock, to the best of my knowledge, and left about nine; the prisoner was there all the time; he and the persons with him were talking about sheep; they had mutton-chops and roasted potatoes for supper - I left them there when I went away.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How many did you leave there? A. Three of their party - I was once put to see if I liked the butcher business, six years ago - I was never a drover- I was out of employ, on the 22d of September - I had been last in Mr. Thompson's employ of Seymour-place, Bond-street, and am now in his service again - I remember the day of the month, because I was there with them - I cannot say what date this is; it is about the 29th, I think - I never saw the prisoner before - I had been there in the afternoon, from two to four o'clock; the prisoner was there then, and they all went to Watson's the butcher, two or three times during that time; they did not go out from seven to nine o'clock.
Q. Did you see another person? A. There was a fourth person, who was rather in liquor, in the afternoon, but when I returned he was not there - I had left him there at four o'clock - I am sure, when I returned at seven o'clock, these three men were sitting drinking and talking to one another - I sat on the prisoner's right, in the same box, and on the same side - I saw George Weller right opposite me - I was near the window; he was opposite; there was a fire - I sat facing the fire; the chops came from Nicholl's, a butcher - Broom went for them, and the butcher brought them - Broom was one of the three - he went for them about eight o'clock, or a little after; they were cooked in a frying-pan - I do not know where it came from; there were a good many people there the latter part of the evening - I cannot say how many - I believe there were people in all the boxes - I knew one Farns, who was there - I cannot say at what time he came- he was there at four, and between seven and nine o'clock; he left before me - I lodge at No. 9, Bond's-place, Edgware-road.
Q. How came they to find you? A. I was coming into the public-house when his brother, and the other men were there; that was just about dark - I had been looking for a place on the 22d of September; it was not wet - I am not certain whether it was fine - I was not wet through, and therefore did not notice whether it was wet at all; the men who sat by me had some roasted potatoes - George Weller had some and Broom, and a man named Elgar bought a penny-worth himself, I believe, and the prisoner and his party had some; a man brought them in - Broom paid for some, but not for all - we had the remains of their potatoes, after they had done; the potatoe-man went to the other boxes, but whether he sold any more I cannot say; he is not here - I did not notice them talking about any thing but the sheep; the man who went away first was tipsy - I saw these men come in about two o'clock; that man was tipsy when he came in; four persons came in, and one was tipsy; they came into the tap-room; the prisoner had a fustian coat and blue apron; he was dressed something like he is now; he has no apron now- I do not know whether the others had aprons; but he was next to me, with a little dog in his arms.
Q. Which of the three cooked the chops? A. A deaf man - Broom, or some such name.
JURY. Q. Who sat next to you on the other side of the prisoner? A. John Elgar.
COURT. Q. You saw a great deal of them? A. Yes; I went home to tea, from four to seven o'clock; I did not set in the same box with them in the afternoon, but I was in the tap-room from two to four o'clock; I will not say I did not step to the door; I only saw four in the party.
Q. If any body else had come to talk about the sheepstealers, you must have seen them? A. Very likely.
JOHN BROOM. I am a drover, and remember the 22d of September - I saw the prisoner that day, at the Bull's Head, public-house, Smithfield. I went with him to the Hope and Anchor, Paddington: we were in and out there the best part of the afternoon - I did not leave till half-past ten o'clock; the prisoner left about twenty minutes past nine - I supped with him, off mutton-chops and baked potatoes; the chops were fried by a man who was with us. I am certain the prisoner did not leave till after nine o'clock, and am certain as to the day - several persons were in the tap-room whom I did not know; I was never there before I appeared at the Mansion-house to give evidence, and so did some others who are here.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What time did you go with him? A. About half-past twelve o'clock; three of us went together; there were four of us at the Hope and Anchor; the fourth man was a drover - he joined us near Baker-street - a young man named Alby, who was one of the four, was tipsy - he is not here - we lost him there; I do not know where he lives; Thompson, a Police-officer, was there - I went for him about three o'clock; we got there about two. I did not go in with the rest, but left them a few minutes before, and then they went with me to a person named Watkins, a few doors from the Sun public-house, where we called first; I staid at Watkins' a very few minutes. I left the officer with Watkins, and he followed us to the Hope and Anchor; we were in and out between two and four o'clock, and staid out five or ten minutes; we might have gone out half a dozen times; we dined before we left town - we had some porter, and I had some gin and spruce at the bar - I do not know whether the others had any; we sat in the left side box when we first went in, and then changed to the right side; the young man left us about half-past four o'clock; we heard of Watkins, and all went out together, and in the course of that walk lost the tipsy man.
Q. Then it is not true that he got up and went away himself? A. No, we lost him in the walk. The prisoner was dressed the same as he is now, except a handkerchief; he had a kind of blood-red handkerchief - there was no other difference; I am certain he had not a smock-frock; he had no apron on, I am sure. The officer belongs to Bow-street - he was here yesterday, and would have come to-day if he was wanted; he was not at the Mansion-house - I met with him at his own house, No. 5, in the same street as the Hope and Anchor; I semember seeing Seller there very well - I cannot say when I first saw him - he was a stranger to me; I saw him in the tap-room; I first saw him in the left-hand side, and then he turned to the right; I think I saw him before four o'clock, but did not notice him till after that. I saw him in the evening; he was drinking porter in the tap-room - we did not drink to each other - I believe he was in the same box towards the evening. I am not able to say how many were in the box - it is a square box; my face was towards the fire - I do not recollect who sat next the prisoner; we had some muttonchops - Nicholls, the butcher, brought them; the prisoner and myself went to order them - I am sure of that; we cooked them by the tap-room fire - William Hill cooked them.
Q. Did any of your party get up to assist him? A. I am not able to say; the lad brought in the frying-pan, I believe; I bought some potatoes; I believe the prisoner and Hill bought some; no more of our company bought any; the man went to the other boxes; I was there some time.
Q. When you came away did you all pay and settle it afterwards, or did one pay? A. We paid between us; I cannot say how much it was, or what my share was; I spent 4s. 6d. or 5s. in the course of the afternoon; I paid part for the prisoner; I do not know whether he paid any; I asked him to walk with me from the Bull's Head, and did not wish him to pay much; the sheep lost I had to pay for, if I could not find them; we found the man, but he ran away from the officer, saying he would go and get the money; I got the money for them next morning; I am sure it was on the 22d of September; I made no memorandum of it; I was questioned about it at the Mansion-house on the Tuesday week; it was rather a heavy day; it mizzled a little as we walked there; it was heavy a great part of the day.
MR. CHURCHER. Q. What time did Thompson go away? A. About ten minutes to six o'clock; he was not there afterwards; I told him he would not be wanted; I have known the prisoner some time in Smithfield, but was never in his company to spend an evening before; I never heard any thing against him; I considered him to bear an honest character.
COURT. Q. How long have you known him? A. Four or five years by sight; I was never in his company before; I never heard of his being in a Court before; I have heard of his being in custody for getting a little tipsy; I never heard any charge against him.
JOHN YOUNG. I am a waiter at the Hope and Anchor. I have seen the prisoner; he was at our house on the 22d of September till twenty minutes past nine o'clock at night; two other persons were with him, a man with one eye, and another man deaf.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What time did they come? A. Between one and two o'clock; I am sure two more came with him; we understood they were in search of a butcher about some sheep; I saw an officer there between four and five o'clock; I do not know when he left, as I had my business to mind; I only saw him when he came in with the three men, between four and five o'clock; they were the only men who came on that business; they were the officer, the prisoner, Broom, and Hill; I cannot say either of them were tipsy; they might be a little in liquor; I saw no tipsy man come in with them - they went in and out, and had mutton-chops in the evening - there were two or three bakers in the tap-room, George Weller and another baker - a great many people were in the tap-room - Saturday is generally a busy night; this was not particularly a busy night - I know it was on the 22d of September, because when they came in they were talking about some sheep, and about Watkins, and I said I had parted from him not five minutes ago - they did not go after him then; this was between two or three o'clock - the officer lives in the same street - he was here last week, and said he had no objection to come forward - they sat behind the door at supper - they had before sat in the opposite box - I cannot say when they changed - they went out between four and five o'clock, then came in, and sat behind the door - Elgar sat in the same box, and partook of some of the victuals - he had some potatoes - I do not know whether any body else sat in the same box - I could not attend to my business, and notice every thing - I cannot say where Weller or Seller sat - Broom sat close to the prisoner - the prisoner had a frock on under his coat - I believe it is called a smock-frock - it is a loose linen garment, which went down low, and covered his breeches - I cannot say what colour it was, but am sure he had one on - he had a red silk handkerchief round his neck - I believe it was a fine day - I did not pay particular attention to it - I go out with beer, but cannot recollect whether it was fine.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was the officer subpoenaed on the part of the prosecution? A. No.
JOHN ELGAR. I am a baker. On Saturday evening, the 22d of September, I was at the Hope and Anchor; I saw the prisoner there that night, sitting with two more men; he wore the coat he has on now, I believe; and I think a red handkerchief - he had a fustian coat and a dog. I think it rained a little in the afternoon; I saw him eat mutton-chops; I went away a little after ten o'clock; he went out, as near as I can tell, at half-past nine o'clock; I am certain he was there.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Are you certain of the day of the month? A. I recollect it was the 22d, because I read the paper that afternoon - I read the paper yesterday - I have not seen one to-day, and cannot tell the date of to-day; I was working for Mary-le-bone parish that day; I have been with a cabinet-maker, and then a cheesemonger, and after that with a baker; I was never employed as a drover- I suppose I have not been at Smithfield market above a dozen times in my life; I believe the prisoner had a blue apron on, but will not swear it - he had a frock on under his coat - I am sure of it - I saw it under his coat - it would prevent my seeing his waistcoat - I think it was a short-frock - I am not quite certain that he had an apron - I think the frock was blue.
DANIEL FARNS. I am a milkman, and live at No. 16, Harrow-street. I was at the Hope and Anchor on Saturday night, the 22d of September, and saw the prisoner there - he had a sort of dirty drab handkerchief on, and a sort of fustian jacket; I did not much notice his dress.
COURT. Q. What time did you go there? A. About seven o'clock, and left about eight - I think it was a fine day.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What makes you remember that it was the 22d? I did not make any particular remark, but the indictment was read to me, which stated it was the 22d, and I recollected the night I had seen him there; I think this is (hesitating) the 31st day of the month - I am sure he had a dirty drab handkerchief, to the best of my recollection.
Q. It was not blood-red? A. I think not.
Q. You have a red handkerchief, was it as red as that? A. No; it might be a mixture of red and drab - it was dirty; he had a great coat on the table before him; I did not notice his having a smock-frock - he had no apron; I did not see his shirt frill; I sat alone in a left hand box, and they on the right; I did not see them eat any thing.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you particularly notice the dress of every man? A. No; I am sure he was there.
DANIEL NICHOLLS. I am a butcher. On Saturday night, the 22d of September, I took same mutton-chops to the Hope and Anchor, about eight o'clock, to the best of my knowledge, I cannot be certain to a few minutes; I saw the prisoner there I am certain.
MR. ADOLPHUS, Q. Did you enter it in your book? A. No; but on that day I had a goose sent up out of the country; we eat it on Sunday - it was not sent for Michaelmas-day, and we were remarking that we should have goose before Michaelmas-day; I had seen the prisoner before in Smithfield.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you observe his dress? A. I think it was the same as he has now, except that he had a coloured handkerchief.
COURT, Q. You were there but a short time? A. Not a minute scarcely; Broom paid me 1s. 1d.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271025-239]
2216. WILLIAM BRUNTON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
RICHARD HULSE. I am in the employ of Messrs. Charles Butler and Richard Hayward, chemists, of Queen-street, Cheapside. On the 18th of July the prisoner came, and produced this note, signed "William Hopkins." I asked if he was to pay for the articles; he said No; I asked if he came from Mr. Hopkins; he said he did, and I gave him 3 lbs. of bergamot for Mr. Hopkins; he took it away. (Order read).
July, 18, 1827.
Please to send by bearer 3 lbs. of essence of bergamot for St. John-street, WILLIAM HOPKINS.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Did he give you the order? A. No; one of the porters brought it to me; I went to him with it.
WILLIAM HOPKINS. I am a tallow-chandler, and live in St. John-street; the prisoner was in my service three years ago; this order was not written by me, or any body in my house; I never sent for the goods, and never had it.
Cross-examined. Q. You have sent for such things? A. No; I have sent goods there, but never had any thing sent to me from there to my knowledge; I have two servants.
GUILTY.
Confined Nine Months.
There was another indictment against the prisoner.
[trialAccount t18271025-240]
2217. ALEXANDER BARR was indicted for a misdemeanor.
HENRY GRAVES. I live in Wood-street, Cheapside. On the 16th of October the prisoner came to my warehouse, and produced a pattern of sewing silk, and asked for 3 lbs. of green sewing silk for Messrs. Dickman and Co., of Newgate-street, who dealt with me; I let him have it; he had been in the habit of coming from them for goods within a very short time before - it was worth 3l. 18s.; he came again for 2 lbs. of silk to two patterns; I had not the precise pattern, and delivered him 3 lbs. 9 ozs. of silk.
MR. CORNELIUS JORDAIN. I am in partnership with Mr. John Dickman; the prisoner was in our employ till the 14th of October; we used to deal with Mr. Graves; we never authorised him to go on the 16th or 24th of October for any goods.
HENRY HONEY. I am an officer, and apprehended the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18271025-241]
2218. HENRY HIND, JOHN LOCK, and JOHN WATSON were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of October, 1 set of chaise harness, value 3l.; 1 saddle, value 15s., and 1 bridle, value 5s., the goods of William Lack.
The prosecutor did not appear. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271025-242]
2219. WILLIAM REYNOLDS was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of October, two bells, value 16s., the goods of Benjamin Gould.
BENJAMIN GOULD. I am a scavenger. I use these bells when I go out with a cart. I saw them safe in a basket, in my stable, in Worship-street, on Saturday night, the 20th of October, and lost them next morning.
ROBERT CHRISTIE. I work for Gould; last Saturday week, between two and three o'clock, I put the bells into a basket, in the stable - the door was not locked, and on Monday they were gone; a man could get in at the wicket, which was not fastened.
JOHN HANDS. I live with Mr. Gould. I saw the bells put away, and missed them on Saturday morning. I do not know the prisoner.
JOSEPH TAYLOR. I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner at seven o'clock on Sunday morning, the 21st of October, in Petticoat-lane, on the City side, endeavouring to sell these bells,(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 36.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18271025-243]
2220. PHILIP HUMPHRIES was indicted for a misdemeanor.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271025-244]
2221. JOSEPH GARDNER was indicted for a misdemeanor.
THOMAS SMITH. I am servant to Henry and Henry Charles Lee , who are butchers, of Leadenhall-market. On the 24th of September the prisoner came to me; I knew him to have lived with Messrs. Angels; he said, "Thomas, I want 10lbs. or 12lbs. of brisket of beef for stewing;" he said he came from Messrs. Angels and wanted it for them; thinking he still lived with them, I gave it to him.
WILLIAM HENRY ANGEL. I carry on business with my father, William Sandal Angel, at Cornhill - the prisoner was in our employ about three years ago: we never sent him for this meat.
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 30.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Month.

2222. JOHN ROSS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271025-246]
2223. WILLIAM BAKER was indicted for wilful and corrupt perjury.
MR. BOLLAND conducted the prosecution.
JOSEPH MACKWAY produced an examined copy of the record of the conviction of John Elson at the last Session, which was read.
JOHN HAINES. I am an officer of this Court. I was present last Sessions at the trial of John Elson, and administered the oath to the defendant, who was a witness.
WILLIAM FIELD (clerk to H. Buckler, short-hand writer of the Court) read his notes of the trial of John Elson, upon which trial the defendant was examined on the prisoner's behalf, and repeatedly swore he heard Mrs. Donald say, the said John Elson was not the man who had robbed her - for the report of the trial, see Seventh Session, page 741.
MARY DONALD. I am the wife of James Donald. On the evening of the 6th of September I was at Bartholomew-fair with him; I stood in front of Richardson's booth, and my reticule was got from me - I saw Elson standing near me at the time, and saw him when he was taken; I never said he was not the man.
Prisoner. Q. What did you say when your husband collared the man? A. I never said a word.
COURT. Q. When your husband collared the man, how near was he to you? A. He had hold of my arm; if I had said any thing. he must have heard it.
JAMES DONALD. I was with my wife on the 6th of September, near Richardson's booth; we were making our way through, and a great crowd made a push; we made our way towards the railing - they surrounded us, and one snatched my wife's reticule - Elson passed me at the time, and I caught him by the skirts of his coat - my wife never at any time said he was not the man.
Prisoner. Q. Did you collar him? A. I seized him, and called for two officers; they collared him.
MRS. DONALD. I saw the man take my reticule.
JAMES DONALD re-examined. He was running, and I caught hold of the skirts of his coat.
BENJAMIN CHARLES MURREY. I am a constable, and was in the fair on the night in question - I saw Elson opposite Richardson's show, and several noted pick pockets with him - I saw the prisoner at a little distance from them - I saw Elson snatch at the reticule; when he was taken, I heard Mrs. Donald say, "That is the man;" as soon as he snatched the reticule, his hand came down with assistance, we got Elson out of the fair - I heard Mrs. Donald say, he was the man more than once; she said,"He took my reticule;" I was there all the time, and never heard her at any time say he was not the man.
WILLIAM BENNS. I am a constable of St. George's, Bloomsbury. I was in the fair on the evening of the 6th of September; I saw Elson first about an hour before this happened, and again between eleven and twelve o'clock, with a gang of pickpockets; I saw him snatch Mrs. Donald's reticule from her left arm; he made a stoop and put it behind him - what became of it, I do not know; I helped to take him to the Compter; I could hear all Mrs. Donald said - she never, at any time, said, in my hearing, that Elson was not the man.
Prisoner. It is a very hard case I should be subpoenaed from my work, and then brought here; here is my subpoena. I should not have come if I could have helped it.
The prisoner received a very good character.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18271025-247]
Fourth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Common Sergeant.
2224. JOHN FISHER was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Parker, on the 27th of October, and stealing 1 pair of breeches, value 1s. 6d., and 1 shirt, value 3s., his property.
JAMES PARKER. I am a silk-weaver, and live in Wilmot-street, Bethnal-green. On the 27th of October I was in my workshop, about ten o'clock in the morning; my daughter called me down, and I saw the prisoner and my son in the passage; I looked down, and saw a pair of breeches and a shirt, which had been locked up in my room - I laid them on the stairs; my son gave me this key; I sent him for an officer, and when he came, I put this key in the door, and opened and shut it several times - I then gave the prisoner into custody.
CHARLES PARKER. I am the prosecutor's son. About ten o'clock in the morning of the 27th of October I came out of my father's house, and passed the prisoner within a few yards of it; I crossed the way, and happened to turn my head; I saw the prisoner put his hand up, as if to open the door - he had his hand upon it; he was in for two or three minutes, and then came out with some things under his arm; I had fastened the door myself, and had not left it three minutes - I had not my eye upon the door all that time; I crossed the road and ran after him - I said he must come back to my father's, which he did, and dropped in the passage a pair of breeches and a shirt; another witness came up to the door with a key, and I saw my father try it to the lock of our room door, which it opened as well as our own - it was picked up near where I took the prisoner; he left the door open.
SARAH PARKER. I am the prosecutor's wife. I was called down by my daughter, and saw the prisoner with this property near him.
BENJAMIN EVE. I live in Smart's-gardens. On Saturday last I was going out of my garden, and saw Charles Parker take hold of the prisoner, who dropped something, I could not see what; I went up to the spot and picked up this key, which must be what he dropped - I saw it tried to Parker's door.
Prisoner. I know nothing about the key.
JAMES ESSEX. I am a constable. I went to the house, and found the prisoner in the passage; he said he was very sorry for what he had done, and he did it through distress.
GUILTY. Aged 40. Of stealing only. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18271025-248]
2225. SARAH CORNISH was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of September, 1 bag, value 2d.; 1 sovereign, and 1 half-crown, the property of James Ausinder from his person.
JAMES AUSINDER. I am a millwright. I was in Kingsland-road on Michaelmas-day; the prisoner came up and put her hand into my left-hand pocket, and took this bag of money; I drew her hand out the first time, but when I drew it out, she put it in a second time, and I missed my purse: I said if she did not return it I would give her in charge; she called out Bill, and four or five men came round: she said, "Bill, this b - r says I have got his purse, but I have no purse of his" - I saw her hand pass to his hand; she then crossed the way, and I gave charge of her: she had asked me for gin - I said it was too late; shesaid I had no money; I said, "Yes, I have," pulling out a sixpence: she said, "Give me that?" she then said,"Come this way, I want to speak to you."
Prisoner. It happened in an empty-house, down a field. Witness. No - it was thirty or forty yards from the road; she took the 6d., and then put her hands round me.
JOHN HOSKINS. I heard a rattle spring; I went to the spot, and found the prosecutor holding the prisoner; he said she had robbed him of a sovereign and half a crown; I searched her, and found but 1s. 6d. and 4d. on her; he at first said it was a sovereign and some silver - he said a man had gone down Mill-row, but it was of no use my going after him, as he was gone some time, and he thought he must have had the money.
JAMES HAWES. I am a watchman. I heard the rattle, ran up, and saw the prosecutor and prisoner on the bridge, in the Kingsland-road; the prisoner was almost stripped - they were searching her; he said he had lost a sovereign and half a crown, and he thought the man who ran away had got it; he was sober.
Prisoner's Defence. He told the Magistrate he had but 6d. in the world.
JAMES AUSINDER. I did not.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271025-249]
2226. MICHAEL RUSSELL was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of October, 1 seal, value 25s., and 3 watch-keys, value 10s., the goods of Abraham Delvalle, from his person.
The prosecutor did not appear. NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18271025-250]
2227. JOHN WALLEN was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 1 box, value 5s.; 3 gowns, value 6s.; 4 petticoats, value 3s.; 2 shifts, value 4s.; 6 pairs of stockings, value 6s.; 6 aprons, value 6s., and 1 apron, value 2s., the goods of Esther Mackreth.
ESTHER MACKRETH. I am single. On the 25th of October I had left my situation to go into the country, and employed Grey to fetch my boxes - he keeps a horse and cart; he received them at No. 4, Charlotte-street, Blackfriars', to take to Crown-court, Goswell-street, but I only received one box there - the other contained the articles stated in the indictment and others.
JAMES GREY. I received two boxes from the prosecutrix on Thursday; as I went along I found my horse lame - I stopped at a farrier's in St. John-street, as I could not get on; I delivered both boxes at the Queen's Head public-house, St. John-lane; I gave them into the prisoner's care, while I took the horse out; he had been in my employ. I returned after the horse was shod, and he and the box were gone.
WILLIAM CATER. I keep the Queen's Head. I remember the prisoner and prosecutor bringing two boxes to my house - he left the prisoner there with two boxes - I saw the prisoner take one away - that was a blue box; he left the other on the table; I took it into the bar for safety: he left my house about six o'clock - he was neither drunk nor sober.
MARY EYRE. I am servant to Mr. West, of Saffron-hill - the prisoner lodged there. On Thursday evening he came into the outer passage, sat the box on the mat, and wanted to take it up stairs; my master was not at home - I said master would not allow any boxes to be taken up - he then took it away, saying he must take it to his sister's.
WILLIAM WATSON. I saw the prisoner on Thursday night, about eleven o'clock, and asked what he had done with the box - he said he had been knocked down, and it was taken from him.
ABRAHAM GRIFFITHS. The prisoner was brought to me at the watch-house, and said he was knocked down in Wilderness-row, and the box taken from him.
Prisoner's Defence. Grey told me to take one of the boxes home; I had been drinking, and found myself rather stupid; I took it to my lodging, thinking to take it home in the morning - the young woman would not take it in; I took it away, and set it down while I stopped for a certain purpose, and it was taken.
JAMES GREY. I did not desire him to take them home, but to take care of them till I returned.
GUILTY. Aged 26.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18271025-251]
2228. THOMAS WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of October, 1 pair of shoes, value 2s., the goods of John Howard.
JAMES WILLIAM BARRIER. I am foreman to John Howard, a bootmaker, of St. Martin's-court. Last Saturday evening, between six and seven o'clock, Groom came and asked if I missed a pair of shoes - I had not missed them - I afterwards saw the prisoner and another come up towards the door - I had some suspicion, and looked them hard in the face; the other man then walked off, and the prisoner followed him - I soon after saw the prisoner and another person with him - I went up to the prisoner, and said, "Walk off, or I will give you in charge" - Groom then came up, collared him, and said, "This is the man who took your shoes" - we have got a great many about the door - I did not miss any, and cannot swear to any being gone, we have so many.
JOHN GROOM. I am a constable, and was stationed in this court. I was coming down the court, between six and seven o'clock in the evening, and saw the prisoner untwist a pair of shoes from Howard's door - I went into an umbrella shop, and watched him and another, who was with him; they waited there a few minutes, and then two ladies went into the shop; the prisoner then took the shoes, gave them to the other person, and another person with a bag, came up close to them, and went away directly down the court; the prisoner and his companion went up the court - I followed the man with the bag, and took him to a public-house; the shoes were not in the bag, but two pairs of ladies' boots - I found where he lived, and what he had was his own - I am quite sure I saw the prisoner take a pair of shoes, and give them to the other person.
Prisoner. Q. Why did you not take me directly? A. Because I went after the man with the bag; the prisoner and the other man came back in a quarter of an hour - I saw Barrier speaking to the prisoner, and said he was the man who stole the shoes - Mr. Howard came out and said there was a pair missing; the other must have taken them away, while I was gone to the public-house with the third man.
JAMES WILLIAM BARRIER. There was a space where a pair had hung - I had none for two or three hours before.
Prisoner's Defence. I was passing in the court, but did not touch the shoes.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18271025-252]
2229. JOHN CROSS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of October, 1 live tame fowl, price 2s., the property of Charles Edlin.
JAMES BEECHY. I am an officer of the Thames Police. On the 24th of October, about half-past six o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner in King David's Fort, with a live fowl, in a smock frock, under his arm - I asked what he had got; he said nothing, but when I found it, he said he bought it of a man for a shilling.
CHARLES EDLIN. I live in Johnson-street. This is my fowl - I know it well, by the top knot being on one side; it was safe between five and six o'clock, fastened up.
Prisoner. Q. You could not swear to it before the Magistrate. A. No, but I have another witness here.
JOHN PENN. I am a wheelwright. I know this fowl; it was mine for four or five weeks - I know it by its head, feather, wing, and every thing - I sold it to the prosecutor - there is not another like it.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought it of a man.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18271025-253]
2230. BENJAMIN CANNON was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of October, 7lbs. of candles, value 4s., the goods of Thomas Shelton.
THOMAS TOTTERDALL. I am in the employ of Thomas Shelton, an oilman, of Lisson-grove, North. Last Saturday morning, between ten and eleven o'clock, I left my barrow and basket at the corner of South-bank, with wood and candles in it. I went to a house, and on returning, the basket and candles were gone: I saw the prisoner about a dozen yards off - I ran after him, and took him with the candles in his hand - he had moved the barrow about twelve yards, and then taken the basket out.
WILLIAM BETTY. I was at the corner of the street, and saw the prisoner move the barrow, and then take the basket; he came towards me; I took hold of him, and asked what he was going to do; he said to take it to a man - he could not tell me who; he had wheeled the barrow about seventy yards.
Prisoner's Defence. A gentleman told me to move it out of the way of his gig.
GUILTY. Aged 68.
Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18271025-254]
2231. JAMES BOLITHO was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of October, 3 shirts, value 1s. 6d.; 1 pinafore, value 4d.; 1 towel, value 2d., and 1 sheet, value 6d., the goods of Thomas Thompson.
MARY THOMPSON. I am wife of Thomas Thompson. I sell vegetables in Spitalfields-market - last Saturday a person brought these things to me from the wash - I put them into my basket, and at half-past eleven o'clock, when I packed up, the prisoner came to help me home with my vegetables - he works in the market - he helped me with my things into the cellar, and I found the basket had been opened, and the bundle gone - he came next morning to take away my cart - I called him aside, and told him of it- he denied it - I asked where he lived - he said no where - he would not tell me where they were - I called the officer, and when he saw his staff he said the things were at his mother's.
BANJAMIN HARRIS. I am an officer. The prisoner denied having the things, but when I took out my staff he owned they were at home - I went with him, and he gave them to me.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 14.
Strongly recommended to Mercy.
Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18271025-255]
2232. ELIZABETH CANNON was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of October, 1 book, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Marsden and Thomas Briscoe.
SECOND COUNT, calling it the property of Thomas Bliss.
THOMAS BLISS. I am sexton of St. Matthew, Bethnal-green - I live in Church-street. On the 29th of October I was in the church porch, the inner door was open, but the outer one closed - I saw the prisoner with this Prayer-book in her hand, with which the duties are done in the church; I have the care of it - Thomas Marsden and Thomas Briscoe are the church wardens; she had brought it from the altar, and was coming out at the west end; I heard the inner door squeak, and saw her lay the book down; she was in a state of drunkenness, and nearly naked; I charged her with it, and she said, "It was not me, it was another woman;" but there was no one else in the church.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not take it to steal it.
GUILTY. Aged 59.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18271025-256]
2233. JAMES WILCOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of October, 56 lbs. weight of iron, value 4s., the goods of Anthony Harvey and William Biggerstaff, his masters.
ANTHONY HARVEY. I am in partnership with William Biggerstaff; we are wheelwrights, and live in West-street, Smithfield - the prisoner was in our employ. On the 27th of October an officer stopped him with these iron bars, which were kept adjoining our shop.
JAMES BEECHEY. I am an officer. I stopped the prisoner going into an old iron shop with this property: I asked what he had got; he said a little old iron, which he had picked up at different times; he at first refused to tell where he lived, but afterwards said he took it from the prosecutors', and hoped they would not hurt him.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Confined Four Months.
